![](images/36e3319f05810104c954c1554c11ff8a5d58ef2dee04ad79d437ff9c6f723897.jpg)  
(d) run time with  $r = 20$  
figure 5: the relative errors of scaledgd, vanilla gd and altmin with respect to the iteration count and run time (in seconds) under different condition numbers  $\kappa = 1, 5, 20$  for matrix sensing with  $n = 200$ , and  $m = 5nr$ . (a, b):  $r = 10$ ; (c, d):  $r = 20$ .

which corresponds to solving two least-squares problems. for matrix completion, the update rule of alternating minimization proceeds as

$$
\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} = \operatorname * {a r g m i n} _ {\boldsymbol {l}} \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\omega} (\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} _ {t} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {y}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2},
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {r} _ {t + 1} = \operatorname * {a r g m i n} _ {\boldsymbol {r}} \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\omega} (\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {y}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2},
$$

which can be implemented more efficiently since each row of  $\mathbf{l}$  (resp.  $\mathbf{r}$ ) can be updated independently via solving a much smaller least-squares problem due to the decomposable structure of the objective function. it is worth noting that, to the best of our knowledge, this most natural variant of alternating minimization for matrix completion still eludes from a provable performance guarantee, nonetheless, we choose it to compare against due to its popularity and excellent empirical performance.

figure 5 plots the relative errors of scaledgd, vanilla gd and alternating minimization (altmin) with respect to the iteration count and run time (in seconds) under different condition numbers  $\kappa = 1,5,20$ ; and similarly, figure 6 plots the corresponding results for matrix completion. it can be seen that, both scaledgd and altmin admit a convergence rate that is independent of the condition number, where the per-iteration complexity of altmin is much higher than that of scaledgd. as expected, the run time of scaledgd only adds a minimal overhead to vanilla gd while being much more robust to ill-conditioning. noteworthy, altmin takes much more time and becomes significantly slower than scaledgd when the rank  $r$  is larger. nonetheless, we emphasize that since the run time is impacted by many factors in terms of problem

![](images/a7cbe14b35b40b2a69ae7d64a61f5b9b88a35837c8904eb3083c32418f615dcf.jpg)  
(a) iteration count with  $r = 10$

![](images/d5447285c7d8b750dd296550f145128bba2f941c387ae17f16ab5e357bd0c5ae.jpg)  
(b) run time with  $r = 10$

![](images/007e778b2db6f9475eac73a47d5fa036eb7561442081e7816ec0ac4e78de97fa.jpg)  
(c) iteration count with  $r = 50$

![](images/77ae889520e791b24217d3fa4b020f1ce0cdc97f2586f2ae17fa1ca6ab0834a5.jpg)  
(d) run time with  $r = 50$  
figure 6: the relative errors of scaledgd, vanilla gd and altmin with respect to the iteration count and run time (in seconds) under different condition numbers  $\kappa = 1,5,20$  for matrix completion with  $n = 1000$ , and  $p = 0.2$ . (a, b):  $r = 10$ ; (c, d):  $r = 50$ .

parameters as well as implementation details, our purpose is to demonstrate the competitive performance of scaledgd over alternatives, rather than claiming it as the state-of-the-art.

# 5 conclusions

this paper proposes scaled gradient descent (scaledgd) for factored low-rank matrix estimation, which maintains the low per-iteration computational complexity of vanilla gradient descent, but offers significant speed-up in terms of the convergence rate with respect to the condition number  $\kappa$  of the low-rank matrix. in particular, we rigorously establish that for low-rank matrix sensing, robust pca, and matrix completion, to reach  $\epsilon$ -accuracy, scaledgd only takes  $o(\log(1/\epsilon))$  iterations without the dependency on the condition number when initialized via the spectral method, under standard assumptions. the key to our analysis is the introduction of a new distance metric that takes into account the preconditioning and unbalancedness of the low-rank factors, and we have developed new tools to analyze the trajectory of scaledgd under this new metric. this work opens up many venues for future research, as we discuss below.

- improved analysis. in this paper, we have focused on establishing the fast local convergence rate. it is interesting to study if the theory developed herein can be further strengthened in terms of sample complexity and the size of basin of attraction. for matrix completion, it will be interesting to see if a similar guarantee continues to hold in the absence of the projection, which will generalize recent works [mwcc19, cll20] that successfully removed these projections for vanilla gradient descent.

- other low-rank recovery problems. besides the problems studied herein, there are many other applications involving the recovery of an ill-conditioned low-rank matrix, such as robust pca with missing data, quadratic sampling, and so on. it is of interest to establish fast convergence rates of scaledgd that are independent of the condition number for these problems as well. in addition, it is worthwhile to explore if a similar preconditioning trick can be useful to problems beyond low-rank matrix estimation. one recent attempt is to generalize scaledgd for low-rank tensor estimation  $\left[\mathrm{tmpb}^{+}21\right]$ .  
- acceleration schemes? as it is evident from our analysis of the general loss case, scaledgd may still converge slowly when the loss function is ill-conditioned over low-rank matrices, i.e.  $\kappa_{f}$  is large. in this case, it might be of interest to combine techniques such as momentum [kc12] from the optimization literature to further accelerate the convergence. in our companion paper [tmc21], we have extended scaledgd to nonsmooth formulations, which possess better curvatures than their smooth counterparts for certain problems.

# a technical lemmas

this section gathers several useful lemmas that will be used in the appendix. throughout all lemmas, we use  $\mathbf{x}_{\star}$  to denote the ground truth low-rank matrix, with its compact svd as  $\mathbf{x}_{\star} = \mathbf{u}_{\star}\mathbf{\sigma}_{\star}\mathbf{v}_{\star}^{\top}$ , and the stacked factor matrix is defined as  $\mathbf{f}_{\star} = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{l}_{\star} \\ \mathbf{r}_{\star} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{u}_{\star}\mathbf{\sigma}_{\star}^{1/2} \\ \mathbf{v}_{\star}\mathbf{\sigma}_{\star}^{1/2} \end{bmatrix}$ .

# a.1 new distance metric

we begin with the investigation of the new distance metric (10), where the matrix  $\mathbf{q}$  that attains the infimum, if exists, is called the optimal alignment matrix between  $\mathbf{f}$  and  $\mathbf{f}_{\star}$ ; see (11). notice that (10) involves a minimization problem over an open set (the set of invertible matrices). hence the minimizer, i.e. the optimal alignment matrix between  $\mathbf{f}$  and  $\mathbf{f}_{\star}$  is not guaranteed to be attained. fortunately, a simple sufficient condition guarantees the existence of the minimizer; see the lemma below.

lemma 9. fix any factor matrix  $\pmb{f} = \left[ \begin{array}{c}\pmb {l}\\ \pmb {r} \end{array} \right]\in \mathbb{r}^{(n_1 + n_2)\times r}$ . suppose that

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} (\boldsymbol {f}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}) = \sqrt {\inf  _ {\boldsymbol {q} \in \mathrm {g l} (r)} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {q} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}} <   \sigma_ {r} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}), \tag {34}
$$

then the minimizer of the above minimization problem is attained at some  $\mathbf{q} \in \mathrm{gl}(r)$ , i.e. the optimal alignment matrix  $\mathbf{q}$  between  $\mathbf{f}$  and  $\mathbf{f}_{\star}$  exists.

proof. in view of the condition (34) and the definition of infimum, one knows that there must exist a matrix  $\bar{q} \in \mathrm{gl}(r)$  such that

$$
\sqrt {\left\| \left(l \bar {q} - l _ {\star}\right) \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(r \bar {q} ^ {- \top} - r _ {\star}\right) \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}} \leq \epsilon \sigma_ {r} (x _ {\star}),
$$

for some  $\epsilon$  obeying  $0 < \epsilon < 1$ . it further implies that

$$
\left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \vee \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \epsilon .
$$

invoke weyl's inequality  $|\sigma_r(\pmb{a}) - \sigma_r(\pmb{b})| \leq \| \pmb{a} - \pmb{b} \|_{\mathrm{op}}$ , and use that  $\sigma_r(\pmb{l}_\star \pmb{\sigma}_\star^{-1/2}) = \sigma_r(\pmb{u}_\star) = 1$  to obtain

$$
\left. \sigma_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) \geq \sigma_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) - \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \geq 1 - \epsilon . \right. \tag {35}
$$

in addition, it is straightforward to verify that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \inf  _ {\boldsymbol {q} \in \operatorname {g l} (r)} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {q} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} (36) \\ = \inf  _ {\boldsymbol {h} \in \operatorname {g l} (r)} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} ^ {- \top} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}. (37) \\ \end{array}
$$

indeed, if the minimizer of the second optimization problem (cf. (37)) is attained at some  $h$ , then  $\bar{q}h$  must be the minimizer of the first problem (36). therefore, from now on, we focus on proving that the minimizer of the second problem (37) is attained at some  $h$ . in view of (36) and (37), one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \inf  _ {\boldsymbol {h} \in \mathrm {g l} (r)} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} ^ {- \top} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} \\ \leq \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

clearly, for any  $\bar{q} h$  to yield a smaller distance than  $\bar{q}$ ,  $h$  must obey

$$
\sqrt {\left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} ^ {- \top} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}} \leq \epsilon \sigma_ {r} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}).
$$

it further implies that

$$
\left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \vee \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} ^ {- \top} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \epsilon .
$$

invoke weyl's inequality  $|\sigma_1(\pmb {a}) - \sigma_1(\pmb {b})|\leq \| \pmb {a} - \pmb {b}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  , and use that  $\sigma_{1}(l_{\star}\sigma_{\star}^{-1 / 2}) = \sigma_{1}(u_{\star}) = 1$  to obtain

$$
\left. \sigma_ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) \leq \sigma_ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq 1 + \epsilon . \right. \tag {38}
$$

combine (35) and (38), and use the relation  $\sigma_r(\pmb{a})\sigma_1(\pmb{b}) \leq \sigma_1(\pmb{ab})$  to obtain

$$
\sigma_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) \sigma_ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) \leq \sigma_ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) \leq \frac {1 + \epsilon}{1 - \epsilon} \sigma_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right).
$$

as a result, one has  $\sigma_{1}(\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{1/2}\pmb{h}\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{-1/2}) \leq \frac{1 + \epsilon}{1 - \epsilon}$ .

similarly, one can show that  $\sigma_{1}(\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{1/2}\pmb{h}^{-\top}\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{-1/2}) \leq \frac{1 + \epsilon}{1 - \epsilon}$ , equivalently,  $\sigma_{r}(\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{1/2}\pmb{h}\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{-1/2}) \geq \frac{1 - \epsilon}{1 + \epsilon}$ . combining the above two arguments reveals that the minimization problem (37) is equivalent to the constrained problem:

$$
\underset {\boldsymbol {h} \in \operatorname {g l} (r)} {\text {m i n i m i z e}} \quad \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} ^ {- \top} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}
$$

$$
\mathrm {s . t .} \quad \frac {1 - \epsilon}{1 + \epsilon} \leq \sigma_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) \leq \sigma_ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) \leq \frac {1 + \epsilon}{1 - \epsilon}.
$$

notice that this is a continuous optimization problem over a compact set. apply the weierstrass extreme value theorem to finish the proof.

with the existence of the optimal alignment matrix in place, the following lemma provides the first-order necessary condition for the minimizer.

lemma 10. for any factor matrix  $\mathbf{f} = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{l} \\ \mathbf{r} \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{r}^{(n_1 + n_2) \times r}$ , suppose that the optimal alignment matrix

$$
\boldsymbol {q} = \operatorname *{argmin}_{\boldsymbol {q}\in \mathrm{gl}(r)}\left\| (\boldsymbol {l}\boldsymbol {q} - \boldsymbol{l}_{\star})\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\star}^{1 / 2}\right\|_{\mathsf{f}}^{2} + \left\| (\boldsymbol {r}\boldsymbol{q}^{-\top} - \boldsymbol{r}_{\star})\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\star}^{1 / 2}\right\|_{\mathsf{f}}^{2}
$$

between  $\mathbf{f}$  and  $f_{\star}$  exists, then  $\mathbf{q}$  obeys

$$
\left(l q\right) ^ {\top} \left(l q - l _ {\star}\right) \sigma_ {\star} = \sigma_ {\star} \left(r q ^ {- \top} - r _ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} r q ^ {- \top}. \tag {39}
$$

proof. expand the squares in the definition of  $q$  to obtain

$$
\boldsymbol {q} = \operatorname * {a r g m i n} _ {\boldsymbol {q} \in \mathrm {g l} (r)} \operatorname {t r} \left((\boldsymbol {l q} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}) ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {l q} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star}\right) + \operatorname {t r} \left((\boldsymbol {r q} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}) ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {r q} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star}\right).
$$

clearly, the first order necessary condition (i.e. the gradient is zero) yields

$$
2 l ^ {\top} \left(l q - l _ {\star}\right) \sigma_ {\star} - 2 q ^ {- \top} \sigma_ {\star} \left(r q ^ {- \top} - r _ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} r q ^ {- \top} = 0,
$$

which implies the optimal alignment criterion (39).

last but not least, we connect the newly proposed distance to the usual frobenius norm in lemma 11, the proof of which is a slight modification to [tbs+16, lemma 5.4] and [gjz17, lemma 41].

lemma 11. for any factor matrix  $\mathbf{f} = \left[ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf{l} \\ \mathbf{r} \end{array} \right] \in \mathbb{r}^{(n_1 + n_2) \times r}$ , the distance between  $\mathbf{f}$  and  $\mathbf{f}_{\star}$  satisfies

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} (\boldsymbol {f}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}) \leq \left(\sqrt {2} + 1\right) ^ {1 / 2} \| \boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}.
$$

proof. suppose that  $\mathbf{x} \coloneqq \mathbf{l}\mathbf{r}^{\top}$  has compact svd as  $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{u}\mathbf{\sigma}\mathbf{v}^{\top}$ . without loss of generality, we can assume that  $\mathbf{f} = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{u}\mathbf{\sigma}^{1/2} \\ \mathbf{v}\mathbf{\sigma}^{1/2} \end{bmatrix}$ , since any factorization of  $\mathbf{l}\mathbf{r}^{\top}$  yields the same distance. introduce two auxiliary matrices  $\bar{\mathbf{f}} \coloneqq \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{u}\mathbf{\sigma}^{1/2} \\ -\mathbf{v}\mathbf{\sigma}^{1/2} \end{bmatrix}$  and  $\bar{\mathbf{f}}_{\star} \coloneqq \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{u}_{\star}\mathbf{\sigma}_{\star}^{1/2} \\ -\mathbf{v}_{\star}\mathbf{\sigma}_{\star}^{1/2} \end{bmatrix}$ . apply the dilation trick to obtain

$$
2 \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & x \\ x ^ {\top} & 0 \end{array} \right] = f f ^ {\top} - \bar {f} \bar {f} ^ {\top}, \quad 2 \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & x _ {\star} \\ x _ {\star} ^ {\top} & 0 \end{array} \right] = f _ {\star} f _ {\star} ^ {\top} - \bar {f} _ {\star} \bar {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top}.
$$

as a result, the squared frobenius norm of  $x - x_{\star}$  is given by

$$
8 \| \boldsymbol {x} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star} \| _ {f} ^ {2} = \left\| \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} - \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top} + \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {\star} \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {f} ^ {2}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} = \left\| \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} ^ {\top} - \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {\star} \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} - 2 \operatorname {t r} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top}\right) \left(\bar {\boldsymbol {f}} \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} ^ {\top} - \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {\star} \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {\star} ^ {\top}\right)\right) \\ = 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {f} ^ {2} + 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {\star} \right\| _ {f} ^ {2} + 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \bar {\boldsymbol {f}} \right\| _ {f} ^ {2} \\ \geq 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we use the facts that  $\| \boldsymbol{f}\boldsymbol{f}^{\top} - \boldsymbol{f}_{\star}\boldsymbol{f}_{\star}^{\top}\|_{\mathsf{f}}^{2} = \| \bar{\boldsymbol{f}}\bar{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\top} - \bar{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\star}\bar{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\star}^{\top}\|_{\mathsf{f}}^{2}$  and  $\boldsymbol{f}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{f}} = \boldsymbol{f}_{\star}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\star} = \mathbf{0}$ .

let  $\pmb{o} \coloneqq \mathrm{sgn}(\pmb{f}^{\top}\pmb{f}_{\star})^{5}$  be the optimal orthonormal alignment matrix between  $\pmb{f}$  and  $\pmb{f}_{\star}$ . denote  $\pmb{\delta} \coloneqq \pmb{fo} - \pmb{f}_{\star}$ . follow the same argument as [tbs+16, lemma 5.14] and [gjz17, lemma 41] to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} 4 \| \boldsymbol {x} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} \geq \left\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} \boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left(2 f _ {\star} ^ {\top} f _ {\star} \delta^ {\top} \delta + (\delta^ {\top} \delta) ^ {2} + 2 (f _ {\star} ^ {\top} \delta) ^ {2} + 4 f _ {\star} ^ {\top} \delta \delta^ {\top} \delta\right) \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left(2 f _ {\star} ^ {\top} f _ {\star} \delta^ {\top} \delta + (\delta^ {\top} \delta + \sqrt {2} f _ {\star} ^ {\top} \delta) ^ {2} + (4 - 2 \sqrt {2}) f _ {\star} ^ {\top} \delta \delta^ {\top} \delta\right) \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left(2 (\sqrt {2} - 1) \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} + (\boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} + \sqrt {2} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta}) ^ {2} + (4 - 2 \sqrt {2}) \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f o} \boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta}\right) \\ \geq \operatorname {t r} \left(4 (\sqrt {2} - 1) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta}\right) = 4 (\sqrt {2} - 1) \left\| (\boldsymbol {f o} - \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {f} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality follows from the facts that  $f_{\star}^{\top}f_{\star} = 2\sigma_{\star}$  and that  $f_{\star}^{\top}fo$  is positive semi-definite. therefore we obtain

$$
\left\| \left(\boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {o} - \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} \leq \left(\sqrt {2} + 1\right) ^ {1 / 2} \| \boldsymbol {x} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}.
$$

this in conjunction with  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb {f},\pmb{f}_{\star})\leq \| (\pmb {f}\pmb {o} - \pmb{f}_{\star})\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{1 / 2}\|_{\mathsf{f}}$  yields the claimed result.

![](images/8dbae36979702b8e1f32c7d5560cc6b6627f7aff79495f483827cf03797239d5.jpg)

# a.2 matrix perturbation bounds

lemma 12. for any  $\pmb{l} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times r}, \pmb{r} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_2 \times r}$ , denote  $\pmb{\delta}_l \coloneqq \pmb{l} - \pmb{l}_{\star}$  and  $\pmb{\delta}_r \coloneqq \pmb{r} - \pmb{r}_{\star}$ . suppose that  $\| \pmb{\delta}_l \pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{-1/2} \|_{\mathrm{op}} \vee \| \pmb{\delta}_r \pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{-1/2} \|_{\mathrm{op}} < 1$ , then one has

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {l} \left(\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {1}{1 - \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}; \tag {40a}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {1}{1 - \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}; \tag {40b}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {l} \left(\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \boldsymbol {u} _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {\sqrt {2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}{1 - \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}; \tag {40c}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - v _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {\sqrt {2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}{1 - \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}. \tag {40d}
$$

proof. we only prove claims (40a) and (40c) on the factor  $\pmb{l}$ , while the claims on the factor  $\pmb{r}$  follow from a similar argument. we start to prove (40a). notice that

$$
\left. \left\| \boldsymbol {l} \left(\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \frac {1}{\sigma_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right)}. \right.
$$

in addition, invoke weyl's inequality to obtain

$$
\sigma_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) \geq \sigma_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) - \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = 1 - \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}},
$$

where we have used the fact that  $\pmb{u}_{\star} = \pmb{l}_{\star}\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{-1/2}$  satisfies  $\sigma_{r}(\pmb{u}_{\star}) = 1$ . combine the preceding two relations to prove (40a).

we proceed to prove (40c). combine  $\pmb{l}_{\star}^{\top}\pmb{u}_{\star} = \pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{1 / 2}$  and  $(i_{n_1} - l(l^\top l)^{-1}l^\top)l = \mathbf{0}$  to obtain the decomposition

$$
\pmb {l} (\pmb {l} ^ {\top} \pmb {l}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \pmb {u} _ {\star} = - \pmb {l} (\pmb {l} ^ {\top} \pmb {l}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top} \pmb {u} _ {\star} + (\pmb {i} _ {n _ {1}} - \pmb {l} (\pmb {l} ^ {\top} \pmb {l}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {l} ^ {\top}) \pmb {\delta} _ {l} \pmb {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}.
$$

the fact that  $l(l^{\top}l)^{-1}\delta_{l}^{\top}u_{\star}$  and  $(i_{n_1} - l(l^\top l)^{-1}l^\top)\delta_l\sigma_\star^{-1 / 2}$  are orthogonal implies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {l} (\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \boldsymbol {u} _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {l} (\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} + \left\| (\boldsymbol {i} _ {n _ {1}} - \boldsymbol {l} (\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top}) \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {l} (\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {i} _ {n _ {1}} - \boldsymbol {l} (\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2}}{(1 - \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}) ^ {2}} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2}}{(1 - \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}) ^ {2}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used (40a) and the fact that  $\| i_{n_1} - l(l^\top l)^{-1}l^\top \|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1$  in the third line.

![](images/ce52db292fbf8ca319d81333c7ef39849a864c55cb539e7ee8ba12fa8a06f95b.jpg)

lemma 13. for any  $\pmb{l} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times r}$ ,  $\pmb{r} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_2 \times r}$ , denote  $\pmb{\delta}_l \coloneqq \pmb{l} - \pmb{l}_{\star}$  and  $\pmb{\delta}_r \coloneqq \pmb{r} - \pmb{r}_{\star}$ , then one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\boldsymbol {l}} \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \left\| \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\boldsymbol {l}} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} \\ \leq \left(1 + \frac {1}{2} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \vee \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}\right)\right) \left(\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

proof. in light of the decomposition  $\mathbf{l}\mathbf{r}^{\top} - \mathbf{x}_{\star} = \pmb{\delta}_{l}\pmb{r}_{\star}^{\top} + \pmb{l}_{\star}\pmb{\delta}_{r}^{\top} + \pmb{\delta}_{l}\pmb{\delta}_{r}^{\top}$  and the triangle inequality, one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\boldsymbol {l}} \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \left\| \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\boldsymbol {l}} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} \\ = \| \delta_ {l} \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \delta_ {r} \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \delta_ {l} \delta_ {r} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used the facts that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} = \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {v} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} = \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}, \quad \mathrm {a n d} \quad \| \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} = \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} = \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}.
$$

this together with the simple upper bound

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} = \frac {1}{2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \frac {1}{2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2}\right) ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} \\ \leq \frac {1}{2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {f} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \frac {1}{2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {f} \\ \leq \frac {1}{2} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \vee \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}\right) \left(\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

finishes the proof.

lemma 14. for any  $l \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times r}$ ,  $r \in \mathbb{r}^{n_2 \times r}$  and any invertible matrices  $q, \bar{q} \in \mathrm{gl}(r)$ , suppose that  $\|(lq - l_\star)\sigma_\star^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \vee \|(rq^{-\top} - r_\star)\sigma_\star^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}} < 1$ , then one has

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \bar {q} ^ {- 1} q \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {\| r (\bar {q} ^ {- \top} - q ^ {- \top}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}{1 - \| (r q ^ {- \top} - r _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}};
$$

$$
\left\| \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \bar {q} ^ {\top} q ^ {- \top} \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \sigma_ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {\| l (\bar {q} - q) \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}{1 - \| (l q - l _ {\star}) \sigma_ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}.
$$

proof. insert  $\pmb{r}^{\top}\pmb{r}(\pmb{r}^{\top}\pmb{r})^{-1}$ , and use the relation  $\| \pmb{a}\pmb{b}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq \| \pmb{a}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\| \pmb{b}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \bar {q} ^ {- 1} q \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} (\bar {q} ^ {- 1} - q ^ {- 1}) \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} q \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {r} (\bar {\boldsymbol {q}} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- \top}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \left\| \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ = \left\| r (\bar {q} ^ {- \top} - q ^ {- \top}) \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\text {o p}} \left\| r q ^ {- \top} ((r q ^ {- \top}) ^ {\top} r q ^ {- \top}) ^ {- 1} \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\text {o p}} \\ \leq \frac {\| \boldsymbol {r} (\bar {\boldsymbol {q}} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- \top}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}{1 - \| (\boldsymbol {r} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last line uses lemma 12.

similarly, insert  $l^{\top}l(l^{\top}l)^{-1}$ , and use the relation  $\| ab\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq \| a\|_{\mathrm{op}}\| b\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \bar {q} ^ {\top} q ^ {- \top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} (\bar {q} ^ {\top} - q ^ {\top}) l ^ {\top} l (l ^ {\top} l) ^ {- 1} q ^ {- \top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {l} (\bar {\boldsymbol {q}} - \boldsymbol {q}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \left\| \boldsymbol {l} (\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- \top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ = \left\| \boldsymbol {l} (\bar {\boldsymbol {q}} - \boldsymbol {q}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \left\| \boldsymbol {l q} ((\boldsymbol {l q}) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l q}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \leq \frac {\left\| l (\bar {q} - q) \sigma_ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}{1 - \left\| (l q - l _ {\star}) \sigma_ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last line uses lemma 12.

![](images/43fff13b1853ae384873ed91f9a592fe2d76a2027b4669f926cb4b7318de504f.jpg)

# a.3 partial frobenius norm

we introduce the partial frobenius norm

$$
\| x \| _ {\mathrm {f}, r} := \sqrt {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \sigma_ {i} ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {x})} = \| \mathcal {p} _ {r} (\boldsymbol {x}) \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \tag {41}
$$

as the  $\ell_2$  norm of the vector composed of the top- $r$  singular values of the matrix  $x$ , or equivalently as the frobenius norm of the rank- $r$  approximation  $\mathcal{p}_r(x)$  defined in (5). it is straightforward to verify that  $\| \cdot \|_{\mathbb{f}, r}$  is a norm; see also [maz16]. the following lemma provides several equivalent and useful characterizations of this partial frobenius norm.

lemma 15. for any  $x \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times n_2}$ , one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {\mathrm {f}, r} = \max  _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n _ {2} \times r}: \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} ^ {\top} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} = \boldsymbol {i} _ {r}} \| \boldsymbol {x} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} (42a) \\ = \max  _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {x}} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n _ {1} \times n _ {2}}: \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {x}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq 1, \operatorname {r a n k} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {x}}) \leq r} | \langle \boldsymbol {x}, \widetilde {\boldsymbol {x}} \rangle | (42b) \\ = \max  _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {r}} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n _ {2} \times r}: \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {r}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq 1} \| \boldsymbol {x} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {r}} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}. (42c) \\ \end{array}
$$

proof. the first representation (42a) follows immediately from the extremal partial trace identity; see [maz16, proposition 4.4], by noticing the following relation

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \sigma_ {i} ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \max _ {\mathbb {v} \subseteq \mathbb {r} ^ {n _ {2}}: \dim (\mathbb {v}) = r} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \mid \mathbb {v}\right) = \max _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n _ {2} \times r}: \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} ^ {\top} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} = \boldsymbol {i} _ {r}} \| \boldsymbol {x} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}.
$$

here the partial trace over a vector space  $\mathbb{v}$  is defined as

$$
\operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \mid \mathbb {v}\right) := \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {i},
$$

where  $\{\widetilde{v}_i\}_{1\leq i\leq r}$  is any orthonormal basis of  $\mathbb{v}$ . the partial trace is invariant to the choice of orthonormal basis and therefore well-defined.

to prove the second representation (42b), for any  $\widetilde{\pmb{x}}\in \mathbb{r}^{n_1\times n_2}$  obeying  $\mathrm{rank}(\widetilde{\pmb{x}})\leq r$  and  $\| \widetilde{x}\|_{\mathsf{f}}\leq 1$  denoting  $\widetilde{\pmb{x}} = \widetilde{\pmb{u}}\widetilde{\pmb{\sigma}}\widetilde{\pmb{v}}^{\top}$  as its compact svd, one has

$$
| \langle x, \widetilde {x} \rangle | = | \langle x, \widetilde {u} \widetilde {\sigma} \widetilde {v} ^ {\top} \rangle | = | \langle x \widetilde {v}, \widetilde {u} \widetilde {\sigma} \rangle | \leq \| x \widetilde {v} \| _ {f} \| \widetilde {u} \widetilde {\sigma} \| _ {f} \leq \| x \| _ {f, r},
$$

where the last inequality follows from (42a). in addition, the maximum in (42b) is attained at  $\widetilde{\pmb{x}} = \mathcal{p}_r(\pmb {x}) / \| \mathcal{p}_r(\pmb {x})\|_{\mathsf{f}}$

to prove the third representation (42c), for any  $\widetilde{\pmb{r}}\in \mathbb{r}^{n_2\times r}$  obeying  $\| \widetilde{\pmb{r}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1$ , combine the variational representation of the frobenius norm and (42b) to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {x}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{r}}\|_{\mathsf{f}} = \max_{\substack{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{l}}\in \mathbb{r}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}:\| \widetilde{\boldsymbol{l}}\|_{\mathsf{f}}\leq 1}}|\langle \boldsymbol {x}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{r}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle | \\ = \max  _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {l}} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n _ {1} \times n _ {2}}: \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {l}} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} \leq 1} | \langle \boldsymbol {x}, \widetilde {\boldsymbol {l}} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {r}} ^ {\top} \rangle | \leq \| \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {\mathsf {f}, r}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality follows from (42b). in addition, the maximum in (42c) is attained at  $\widetilde{\pmb{r}} = \pmb{v}$ , where  $\pmb{v}$  denotes the top- $r$  right singular vectors of  $\pmb{x}$ .

remark 3. for self-completeness, we also provide a detailed proof of the first representation (42a). this proof is inductive on  $r$ . when  $r = 1$ , we have

$$
\sigma_ {1} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \| \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {v} _ {1} \| _ {2} = \max  _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n _ {2}}: \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} \| _ {2} = 1} \| \boldsymbol {x} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} \| _ {2},
$$

where  $\pmb{v}_1$  denotes the top right singular vector of  $\pmb{x}$ . assume that the statement holds for  $\| \cdot \|_{\mathbb{f},r - 1}$ . now consider  $\| \cdot \|_{\mathbb{f},r}$ . for any  $\widetilde{\pmb{v}} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_2 \times r}$  such that  $\widetilde{\pmb{v}}^{\top} \widetilde{\pmb{v}} = \pmb{i}_r$ , we can first pick  $\widetilde{v}_2, \ldots, \widetilde{v}_r$  as a set of orthonormal vectors in the column space of  $\widetilde{\pmb{v}}$  that are orthogonal to  $\pmb{v}_1$ , and then pick  $\widetilde{v}_1$  via the gram-schmidt process, so that  $\{\widetilde{v}_i\}_{i=1}^r$  provides an orthonormal basis of the column space of  $\widetilde{\pmb{v}}$ . further, by the orthogonality of  $\widetilde{\pmb{v}}$ , there exists an orthonormal matrix  $\pmb{o}$  such that

$$
\widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} = [ \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {1}, \dots , \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {r} ] \boldsymbol {o}.
$$

combining this formula with the induction hypothesis yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| x \widetilde {v} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} = \| x [ \widetilde {v} _ {1}, \dots , \widetilde {v} _ {r} ] \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} \\ = \| \boldsymbol {x} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {x} [ \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {2}, \dots , \widetilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {r} ] \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} \\ = \| \boldsymbol {x} \tilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| (\boldsymbol {x} - \mathcal {p} _ {1} (\boldsymbol {x})) [ \tilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {2}, \dots , \tilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {r} ] \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} \\ \leq \sigma_ {1} ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {x}) + \| \boldsymbol {x} - \mathcal {p} _ {1} (\boldsymbol {x}) \| _ {\mathrm {f}, r - 1} ^ {2} \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \sigma_ {i} ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \| \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {\mathsf {f}, r} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first line holds since  $\pmb{o}$  is orthonormal, the third line holds since  $\mathcal{p}_1(\pmb {x})[\widetilde{\pmb{v}}_2,\dots ,\widetilde{\pmb{v}}_r] = \mathbf{0}$ , the fourth line follows from the induction hypothesis, and the last line follows from the definition (41). in addition, the maximum in (42a) is attained at  $\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}} = \boldsymbol{v}$ , where  $\boldsymbol{v}$  denotes the top- $r$  right singular vectors of  $\boldsymbol{x}$ . this finishes the proof.

recall that  $\mathcal{p}_r(\pmb {x})$  denotes the best rank-  $r$  approximation of  $\pmb{x}$  under the frobenius norm. it turns out that  $\mathcal{p}_r(\pmb {x})$  is also the best rank-  $r$  approximation of  $\pmb{x}$  under the partial frobenius norm  $\| \cdot \|_{\mathbb{f},r}$ . this claim is formally stated below; see also [maz16, theorem 4.21].

lemma 16. fix any  $x \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times n_2}$  and recall the definition of  $\mathcal{p}_r(x)$  in (5). one has

$$
\mathcal{p}_{r}(\boldsymbol {x}) = \operatorname *{argmin}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\in \mathbb{r}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}:\mathrm{rank}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}})\leq r}\| \boldsymbol {x} - \widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\|_{\mathsf{f},r}.
$$

proof. for any  $\widetilde{\pmb{x}}$  of rank at most  $r$ , invoke weyl's inequality to obtain  $\sigma_{r + i}(\pmb {x})\leq \sigma_i(\pmb {x} - \widetilde{\pmb{x}}) + \sigma_{r + 1}(\widetilde{\pmb{x}}) = \sigma_i(\pmb {x} - \widetilde{\pmb{x}})$ , for  $i = 1,\ldots ,r$ . thus one has

$$
\| \boldsymbol {x} - \mathcal {p} _ {r} (\boldsymbol {x}) \| _ {\mathrm {f}, r} ^ {2} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \sigma_ {r + i} ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {x}) \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \sigma_ {i} ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {x} - \widetilde {\boldsymbol {x}}) = \| \boldsymbol {x} - \widetilde {\boldsymbol {x}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}, r} ^ {2}.
$$

the proof is finished by observing that the rank of  $\mathcal{p}_r(\pmb {x})$  is at most  $r$ .

# b proof for low-rank matrix factorization

# b.1 proof of proposition 2

the gradients of  $\mathcal{l}(\pmb {f})$  in (29) with respect to  $\pmb{l}$  and  $\pmb{r}$  are given as

$$
\nabla_ {l} \mathcal {l} (\boldsymbol {f}) = \left(\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {r}, \quad \nabla_ {\boldsymbol {r}} \mathcal {l} (\boldsymbol {f}) = \left(\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l},
$$

which can be used to compute the hessian with respect to  $\pmb{l}$  and  $\pmb{r}$ . writing for the vectorized variables, the hessians are given as

$$
\nabla_ {\boldsymbol {l}, \boldsymbol {l}} ^ {2} \mathcal {l} (\boldsymbol {f}) = \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) \otimes \boldsymbol {i} _ {n _ {1}}, \quad \nabla_ {\boldsymbol {r}, \boldsymbol {r}} ^ {2} \mathcal {l} (\boldsymbol {f}) = \left(\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l}\right) \otimes \boldsymbol {i} _ {n _ {2}}.
$$

viewed in the vectorized form, the scaledgd update in (3) can be rewritten as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v e c} \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1}\right) = \operatorname {v e c} \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t}\right) - \eta \left(\left(\boldsymbol {r} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} _ {t}\right) ^ {- 1} \otimes \boldsymbol {i} _ {n _ {1}}\right) \operatorname {v e c} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t} \boldsymbol {r} _ {t} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {r} _ {t}\right) \\ = \operatorname {v e c} (\boldsymbol {l} _ {t}) - \eta \left(\nabla_ {\boldsymbol {l}, \boldsymbol {l}} ^ {2} \mathcal {l} (\boldsymbol {f} _ {t})\right) ^ {- 1} \operatorname {v e c} \left(\nabla_ {\boldsymbol {l}} \mathcal {l} (\boldsymbol {f} _ {t})\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v e c} \left(\boldsymbol {r} _ {t + 1}\right) = \operatorname {v e c} \left(\boldsymbol {r} _ {t}\right) - \eta \left(\left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l} _ {t}\right) ^ {- 1} \otimes \boldsymbol {i} _ {n _ {2}}\right) \operatorname {v e c} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t} \boldsymbol {r} _ {t} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l} _ {t}\right) \\ = \operatorname {v e c} \left(\boldsymbol {r} _ {t}\right) - \eta \left(\nabla_ {\boldsymbol {r}, \boldsymbol {r}} ^ {2} \mathcal {l} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {t}\right)\right) ^ {- 1} \operatorname {v e c} \left(\nabla_ {\boldsymbol {r}} \mathcal {l} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {t}\right)\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

# b.2 proof of theorem 5

the proof is inductive in nature. more specifically, we intend to show that for all  $t \geq 0$ ,

1.  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb {f}_t,\pmb {f}_\star)\leq (1 - 0.7\eta)^t\mathrm{dist}(\pmb {f}_0,\pmb {f}_\star)\leq 0.1(1 - 0.7\eta)^t\sigma_r(\pmb {x}_\star)$  , and

2. the optimal alignment matrix  $q_{t}$  between  $f_{t}$  and  $f_{\star}$  exists.

for the base case, i.e.  $t = 0$ , the first induction hypothesis trivially holds, while the second also holds true in view of lemma 9 and the assumption that  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb{f}_0, \pmb{f}_{\star}) \leq 0.1 \sigma_r(\pmb{x}_{\star})$ . we therefore concentrate on the induction step. suppose that the  $t$ -th iterate  $\pmb{f}_t$  obeys the aforementioned induction hypotheses. our goal is to show that  $\pmb{f}_{t+1}$  continues to satisfy those.

for notational convenience, denote  $\pmb{l} \coloneqq \pmb{l}_t\pmb{q}_t$ ,  $\pmb{r} \coloneqq \pmb{r}_t\pmb{q}_t^{-\top}$ ,  $\delta_l \coloneqq \pmb{l} - \pmb{l}_{\star}$ ,  $\delta_r \coloneqq \pmb{r} - \pmb{r}_{\star}$ , and  $\epsilon \coloneqq 0.1$ . by the definition of  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb{f}_{t + 1},\pmb{f}_{\star})$ , one has

$$
\left. \operatorname {d i s t} ^ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {t + 1}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}\right) \leq \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}, \right. \tag {43}
$$

where we recall that  $q_{t}$  is the optimal alignment matrix between  $\pmb{f}_{t}$  and  $\pmb{f}_{\star}$ . utilize the scaledgd update rule (30) and the decomposition  $\pmb {l}\pmb{r}^{\top} - \pmb{x}_{\star} = \pmb{\delta}_{l}\pmb{r}^{\top} + \pmb{l}_{\star}\pmb{\delta}_{r}^{\top}$  to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(l _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} - l _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} = \left(\boldsymbol {l} - \eta \left(\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} - l _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \\ = \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} - \eta \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \\ = (1 - \eta) \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \eta \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

as a result, one can expand the first square in (43) as

$$
\left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} = (1 - \eta) ^ {2} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right) - 2 \eta (1 - \eta) \underbrace {\operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right)} _ {\mathfrak {m} _ {1}}
$$

$$
+ \eta^ {2} \underbrace {\left\| \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}} _ {\mathfrak {m} _ {2}}. \tag {44}
$$

the first term  $\mathrm{tr}(\pmb{\delta}_l\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}\pmb{\delta}_l^\top)$  is closely related to  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb{f}_t,\pmb{f}_{\star})$ , and hence our focus will be on relating  $\mathfrak{m}_1$  and  $\mathfrak{m}_2$  to  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb{f}_t,\pmb{f}_{\star})$ . we start with the term  $\mathfrak{m}_1$ . since  $\pmb{l}$  and  $\pmb{r}$  are aligned with  $\pmb{l}_{\star}$  and  $\pmb{r}_{\star}$ , lemma 10 tells that  $\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}\pmb{\delta}_l^\top\pmb{l} = \pmb{r}^\top\pmb{\delta}_r\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}$ . this together with  $\pmb{l}_{\star} = \pmb{l} - \pmb{\delta}_l$  allows us to rewrite  $\mathfrak{m}_1$  as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathfrak {m} _ {1} = \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) - \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\boldsymbol {r}} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\boldsymbol {r}} ^ {\top}\right) - \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\boldsymbol {l}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\boldsymbol {l}} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\boldsymbol {r}} ^ {\top}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

moving on to  $\mathfrak{m}_2$ , we can utilize the fact  $l_{\star}^{\top}l_{\star} = \sigma_{\star}$  and the decomposition  $\pmb{\sigma}_{\star} = \pmb{r}^{\top}\pmb{r} - (\pmb{r}^{\top}\pmb{r} - \pmb{\sigma}_{\star})$  to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathfrak {m} _ {2} = \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) - \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

putting  $\mathfrak{m}_1$  and  $\mathfrak{m}_2$  back to (44) yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} = (1 - \eta) ^ {2} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right) - \eta (2 - 3 \eta) \underbrace {\operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right)} _ {\widetilde {\mathfrak {f}} _ {1}} \\ + 2 \eta (1 - \eta) \underbrace {\operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right)} _ {\mathfrak {f} _ {2}} \\ - \eta^ {2} \underbrace {\operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star}) (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right)} _ {\mathfrak {f} _ {3}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

in what follows, we will control the three terms  $\mathfrak{f}_1,\mathfrak{f}_2$  and  $\mathfrak{f}_3$  separately.

1. notice that  $\mathfrak{f}_1$  is the inner product of two positive semi-definite matrices  $r(r^{\top}r)^{-1}r^{\top}$  and  $\delta_r\sigma_\star \delta_r^\top$ . consequently we have  $\mathfrak{f}_1 \geq 0$ .  
2. to control  $\mathfrak{f}_2$ , we need certain control on  $\| \pmb{\delta}_l \pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{-1/2} \|_{\mathrm{op}}$  and  $\| \pmb{\delta}_r \pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{-1/2} \|_{\mathrm{op}}$ . the first induction hypothesis

$$
\mathrm {d i s t} (\boldsymbol {f} _ {t}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}) = \sqrt {\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}} \leq \epsilon \sigma_ {r} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {\star})
$$

together with the relation  $\| ab\|_{\mathsf{f}}\geq \| a\|_{\mathsf{f}}\sigma_r(b)$  tells that

$$
\sqrt {\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}} \sigma_ {r} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}) \leq \epsilon \sigma_ {r} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}).
$$

in light of the relation  $\| a\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq \| a\|_{\mathrm{f}}$  , this further implies

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \vee \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \epsilon . \tag {45}
$$

invoke lemma 12 to see

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {1}{1 - \epsilon}.
$$

with these consequences, one can bound  $|\mathfrak{f}_2|$  by

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \mathfrak {f} _ {2} | = \left| \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2}\right) \right| \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\text {o p}} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2}\right) \\ \leq \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \left\| \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right) \\ \leq \frac {\epsilon}{1 - \epsilon} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

3. similarly, one can bound  $|\mathfrak{f}_3|$  by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathfrak {f} _ {3} \right| \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \\ \leq \frac {1}{(1 - \epsilon) ^ {2}} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

further notice that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r}) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \leq 2 \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \\ \leq 2 \epsilon + \epsilon^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

take the preceding two bounds together to arrive at

$$
| \mathfrak {f} _ {3} | \leq \frac {2 \epsilon + \epsilon^ {2}}{(1 - \epsilon) ^ {2}} \mathrm {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right).
$$

combining the bounds for  $\mathfrak{f}_1, \mathfrak{f}_2, \mathfrak{f}_3$ , one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} = \left\| (1 - \eta) \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \eta \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \\ \leq \left((1 - \eta) ^ {2} + \frac {2 \epsilon}{1 - \epsilon} \eta (1 - \eta)\right) \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right) + \frac {2 \epsilon + \epsilon^ {2}}{(1 - \epsilon) ^ {2}} \eta^ {2} \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right). \tag {46} \\ \end{array}
$$

a similarly bound holds for the second square  $\| (\pmb{r}_{t + 1}\pmb{q}_t - \pmb{r}_\star)\pmb{\sigma}_\star^{1 / 2}\|_{\mathsf{f}}^2$  in (43). therefore we obtain

$$
\left\| (\pmb {l} _ {t + 1} \pmb {q} _ {t} - \pmb {l} _ {\star}) \pmb {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| (\pmb {r} _ {t + 1} \pmb {q} _ {t} ^ {- \top} - \pmb {r} _ {\star}) \pmb {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} \leq \rho^ {2} (\eta ; \epsilon) \mathrm {d i s t} ^ {2} (\pmb {f} _ {t}, \pmb {f} _ {\star}),
$$

where we identify

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} ^ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {t}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}\right) = \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right) + \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \tag {47}
$$

and the contraction rate  $\rho^2 (\eta ;\epsilon)$  is given by

$$
\rho^ {2} (\eta ; \epsilon) := (1 - \eta) ^ {2} + \frac {2 \epsilon}{1 - \epsilon} \eta (1 - \eta) + \frac {2 \epsilon + \epsilon^ {2}}{(1 - \epsilon) ^ {2}} \eta^ {2}.
$$

with  $\epsilon = 0.1$  and  $0 < \eta \leq 2/3$ , one has  $\rho(\eta; \epsilon) \leq 1 - 0.7\eta$ . thus we conclude that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {t + 1}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}\right) \leq \sqrt {\left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}} \\ \leq (1 - 0. 7 \eta) \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {t}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}\right) \\ \leq (1 - 0. 7 \eta) ^ {t + 1} \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {0}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}\right) \leq (1 - 0. 7 \eta) ^ {t + 1} 0. 1 \sigma_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

this proves the first induction hypothesis. the existence of the optimal alignment matrix  $q_{t + 1}$  between  $f_{t + 1}$  and  $f_{\star}$  is assured by lemma 9, which finishes the proof for the second hypothesis.

so far, we have demonstrated the first conclusion in the theorem. the second conclusion is an easy consequence of lemma 13 as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {l} _ {t} \boldsymbol {r} _ {t} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} \leq \left(1 + \frac {\epsilon}{2}\right) \left(\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}\right) \\ \leq \left(1 + \frac {\epsilon}{2}\right) \sqrt {2} \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {t}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}\right) \tag {48} \\ \leq 1. 5 \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {t}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the second line follows from the elementary inequality  $a + b \leq \sqrt{2(a^2 + b^2)}$  and the expression of  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb{f}_t, \pmb{f}_{\star})$  in (47). the proof is now completed.

# c proof for low-rank matrix sensing

we start by recording a useful lemma.

lemma 17 ([cp11]). suppose that  $\mathcal{a}(\cdot)$  obeys the  $2r$ -rip with a constant  $\delta_{2r}$ . then for any  $\pmb{x}_1, \pmb{x}_2 \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times n_2}$  of rank at most  $r$ , one has

$$
| \langle \mathcal {a} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}), \mathcal {a} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {2}) \rangle - \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {2} \rangle | \leq \delta_ {2 r} \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {1} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {2} \| _ {\mathrm {f}},
$$

which can be stated equivalently as

$$
\left| \operatorname {t r} \left(\left(\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}\right) \boldsymbol {x} _ {2} ^ {\top}\right) \right| \leq \delta_ {2 r} \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {1} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}. \tag {49}
$$

as a simple corollary, one has that for any matrix  $\pmb{r} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_2 \times r}$ :

$$
\left\| \left(\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \delta_ {2 r} \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {1} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \| \boldsymbol {r} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}. \tag {50}
$$

this is due to the fact that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} = \max  _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {l}}: \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {l}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq 1} \operatorname {t r} \left(\left(\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {l}} ^ {\top}\right) \\ \leq \max  _ {\tilde {\boldsymbol {l}}: \| \tilde {\boldsymbol {l}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq 1} \delta_ {2 r} \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {1} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \| \tilde {\boldsymbol {l}} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \\ \leq \delta_ {2 r} \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {1} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \| \boldsymbol {r} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the first line follows from the variational representation of the frobenius norm, the second line follows from (49), and the last line follows from the relation  $\| \boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}\|_{\mathsf{f}}\leq \| \boldsymbol {a}\|_{\mathsf{f}}\| \boldsymbol {b}\|_{\mathsf{op}}$

# c.1 proof of lemma 1

the proof mostly mirrors that in section b.2. first, in view of the condition  $\mathrm{dist}(f_t,f_\star)\leq 0.1\sigma_r(x_\star)$  and lemma 9, one knows that  $q_{t}$ , the optimal alignment matrix between  $f_{t}$  and  $f_{\star}$  exists. therefore, for notational convenience, denote  $\pmb {l}:= \pmb {l}_t\pmb {q}_t$ $\pmb {r} := \pmb {r}_t\pmb {q}_t^{-\top}$ $\delta_l\coloneqq l - l_\star$ $\delta_r\coloneqq r - r_\star$  , and  $\epsilon \coloneqq 0.1$  similar to the derivation in (45), we have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \vee \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \epsilon . \tag {51}
$$

the conclusion  $\| l_t r_t^\top - x_\star \|_{\mathbb{f}} \leq 1.5 \, \mathrm{dist}(f_t, f_\star)$  is a simple consequence of lemma 13; see (48) for a detailed argument. from now on, we focus on proving the distance contraction.

with these notations in place, we have by the definition of  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb{f}_{t + 1},\pmb{f}_{\star})$  that

$$
\left. \operatorname {d i s t} ^ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {t + 1}, \boldsymbol {f} _ {\star}\right) \leq \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {r} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} ^ {- \top} - \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}. \right. \tag {52}
$$

apply the update rule (15) and the decomposition  $\pmb{l}\pmb{r}^{\top} - \pmb{x}_{\star} = \pmb{\delta}_{l}\pmb{r}^{\top} + \pmb{l}_{\star}\pmb{\delta}_{r}^{\top}$  to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} = \left(\boldsymbol {l} - \eta \mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} \left(\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \\ = \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} - \eta (\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} - \eta (\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}) (\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \\ = (1 - \eta) \pmb {\delta} _ {l} \pmb {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \eta \pmb {l} _ {\star} \pmb {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \pmb {r} (\pmb {r} ^ {\top} \pmb {r}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \eta (\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}) (\pmb {l} \pmb {r} ^ {\top} - \pmb {x} _ {\star}) \pmb {r} (\pmb {r} ^ {\top} \pmb {r}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

this allows us to expand the first square in (52) as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {q} _ {t} - \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} = \underbrace {\left\| \left(1 - \eta\right) \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} - \eta \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}} _ {\mathfrak {s} _ {1}} \\ - 2 \eta (1 - \eta) \underbrace {\operatorname {t r} \left((\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}) (\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right)} _ {\mathfrak {s} _ {2}} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} + 2 \eta^ {2} \underbrace {\operatorname {t r} \left((\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}) (\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} ^ {\top}\right)} _ {\mathfrak {s} _ {3}} \\ + \eta^ {2} \underbrace {\left\| (\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}) (\boldsymbol {l} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\star}) \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} _ {\mathfrak {s} _ {4}} \\ \end{array}
$$

in what follows, we shall control the four terms separately, of which  $\mathfrak{s}_1$  is the main term, and  $\mathfrak{s}_2,\mathfrak{s}_3$  and  $\mathfrak{s}_4$  are perturbation terms.

1. notice that the main term  $\mathfrak{s}_1$  has already been controlled in (46) under the condition (51). it obeys

$$
\mathfrak {s} _ {1} \leq \left((1 - \eta) ^ {2} + \frac {2 \epsilon}{1 - \epsilon} \eta (1 - \eta)\right) \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \frac {2 \epsilon + \epsilon^ {2}}{(1 - \epsilon) ^ {2}} \eta^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2}.
$$

2. for the second term  $\mathfrak{s}_2$ , decompose  $\boldsymbol{l}\boldsymbol{r}^{\top} - \boldsymbol{x}_{\star} = \boldsymbol{\delta}_{l}\boldsymbol{r}_{\star}^{\top} + \boldsymbol{l}_{\star}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{r}^{\top} + \boldsymbol{\delta}_{l}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{r}^{\top}$  and apply the triangle inequality to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathfrak {s} _ {2} \right| = \left| \operatorname {t r} \left(\left(\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right) \right| \\ \leq \left| \operatorname {t r} \left(\left(\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} ^ {\top}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right) \right| \\ + \left| \operatorname {t r} \left((\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}) \left(\boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right) \right| \\ + \left| \operatorname {t r} \left(\left(\mathcal {a} ^ {*} \mathcal {a} - \mathcal {i}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top}\right) \boldsymbol {r} \left(\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top}\right) \right|. \\ \end{array}
$$

invoke lemma 17 to further obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathfrak {s} _ {2} \right| \leq \delta_ {2 r} \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \left\| \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}}\right) \left\| \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathsf {f}} \\ \leq \delta_ {2 r} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}\right) \left\| \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second line follows from the relation  $\| ab\|_{\mathsf{f}}\leq \| a\|_{\mathsf{op}}\| b\|_{\mathsf{f}}$  . take the condition (51) and lemmas 12 and 13 together to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {r} (\boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {1}{1 - \epsilon}; \\ \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {r} _ {\star} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \boldsymbol {l} _ {\star} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} ^ {\top} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} \leq \left(1 + \frac {\epsilon}{2}\right) \left(\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

these consequences further imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \mathfrak {s} _ {2} | \leq \frac {\delta_ {2 r} (2 + \epsilon)}{2 (1 - \epsilon)} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}\right) \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} \\ = \frac {\delta_ {2 r} (2 + \epsilon)}{2 (1 - \epsilon)} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {r} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\star} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathsf {f}}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

for the term  $\| \pmb{\delta}_l\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{1 / 2}\|_{\mathbb{f}}\| \pmb{\delta}_r\pmb{\sigma}_{\star}^{1 / 2}\|_{\mathbb{f}}$  we can apply the elementary inequality  $2ab\leq a^{2} + b^{2}$  to see

$$
